1000 JOINT CANADA-USA PROJECTS CANADA COULD USE AS TRADE-NEGOTIATION LEVERAGE
Generated August 26, 2026

Scope note: These are proposed, expandable, or negotiable bilateral projects and project families. 'Leverage' here means using future Canadian approvals, investment, procurement, financing, capacity expansion, preferred access, or accelerated cooperation as bargaining assets. Existing essential services, emergency aid, treaty obligations, and safety-critical operations should not be deliberately disrupted.

CATEGORY 1: CROSS-BORDER ELECTRICITY & GRID RESILIENCE
==============================================================================
1. Quebec-New England Hydro Intertie Expansion
   - Canadian lever: Canada can make expansion, long-term power contracts, storage access, and permitting priority contingent on a broader reciprocal trade package. For this project, Ottawa and the relevant province could prioritize the hydro intertie expansion in the Quebec-New England corridor.
   - U.S. value: The U.S. gains firmer capacity, winter reliability, lower congestion risk, and access to dispatchable Canadian electricity where grids are tightly linked. The Quebec-New England geography gives the benefit a concrete regional constituency rather than leaving it as an abstract federal promise.
   - Bargaining use: Use phased capacity reservations, procurement access, and milestone-based approvals as reversible negotiating carrots without interrupting existing essential service. Put the project's next funding, permitting, procurement, or capacity milestone into a written reciprocal implementation schedule.

   - Canadian lever: Canada can condition new incentives, plant expansions, procurement programs, and battery-chain investments on durable tariff treatment for Canadian vehicles and parts. For this project, Ottawa and the relevant province could prioritize the ev battery supply corridor in the Ontario-Michigan corridor.
   - U.S. value: U.S. automakers benefit from integrated Canadian assembly, tooling, minerals, components, and testing capacity that is costly to duplicate. The Ontario-Michigan geography gives the benefit a concrete regional constituency rather than leaving it as an abstract federal promise.
   - Bargaining use: Structure the project around reciprocal rules-of-origin treatment, tariff ceilings, and investment guarantees so both sides gain when the integrated supply chain stays intact. CATEGORY 5: OIL, GAS, HYDROGEN & PETROCHEMICALS
==============================================================================
201. Alberta-Montana Methane-Reduction Partnership
   - Canadian lever: Canada can use approvals, new export capacity, carbon-management infrastructure, and long-term supply arrangements as positive bargaining chips. 401. Quebec-New England Sustainable Aviation Fuel Corridor
   - Canadian lever: Canada can pair aerospace investment, testing access, research funding, and procurement opportunities with requests for tariff relief and reciprocal certification treatment. For this project, Ottawa and the relevant province could prioritize the sustainable aviation fuel corridor in the Quebec-New England corridor.
   - U.S. value: The U.S. gains specialized aerospace manufacturing, cold-weather testing, satellite infrastructure, and access to Canadian aviation expertise. The Quebec-New England geography gives the benefit a concrete regional constituency rather than leaving it as an abstract federal promise.
   - Bargaining use: Use joint venture approvals, research calls, and future procurement tranches as negotiable incentives rather than disrupting existing safety-critical cooperation. Put the project's next funding, permitting, procurement, or capacity milestone into a written reciprocal implementation schedule.

651. British Columbia-Washington Joint Wildfire Airbase
   - Canadian lever: Canada can propose new shared bases, equipment pools, research programs, and manufacturing capacity as part of a broader economic-security bargain. For this project, Ottawa and the relevant province could prioritize the joint wildfire airbase in the British Columbia-Washington corridor.
   - U.S. value: The U.S. gains faster access to aircraft, crews, forecasting, equipment, and northern surge capacity during increasingly severe disaster seasons. The British Columbia-Washington geography gives the benefit a concrete regional constituency rather than leaving it as an abstract federal promise.
   - Bargaining use: Ring-fence life-saving emergency aid from retaliation, but use optional capital expansion, procurement, and permanent basing decisions as negotiating incentives. 901. Ontario-Great Lakes Quantum Research Consortium
   - Canadian lever: Canada can direct new bilateral research funds, test facilities, talent programs, and demonstration sites toward areas where U.S. institutions strongly benefit. For this project, Ottawa and the relevant province could prioritize the quantum research consortium in the Ontario-Great Lakes corridor.
   - U.S. value: The U.S. gains access to Canadian researchers, infrastructure, Arctic environments, datasets, and complementary public R&D investment. The Ontario-Great Lakes geography gives the benefit a concrete regional constituency rather than leaving it as an abstract federal promise.
   - Bargaining use: Use new grant rounds, shared facilities, and commercialization programs as soft leverage tied to stable research mobility and industrial market access. Put the project's next funding, permitting, procurement, or capacity milestone into a written reciprocal implementation schedule.

   - Bargaining use: Deploy these as state- and region-facing carrots, offering new campaigns and infrastructure once reciprocal trade measures are implemented. Put the project's next funding, permitting, procurement, or capacity milestone into a written reciprocal implementation schedule.

==============================================================================
USE PRINCIPLES
==============================================================================
1. Prefer carrots with an on-ramp: accelerated permitting, new capacity, co-investment, procurement access, research access, and long-term contracts.
2. Target future or incremental benefits rather than interrupting existing essential flows.
3. Concentrate offers where U.S. states, workers, farmers, manufacturers, utilities, or governors can see a direct local gain.
4. Make concessions reciprocal, measurable, time-bound, and reversible if agreed trade treatment is withdrawn.
5. Bundle projects into sector packages rather than negotiating 1,000 projects individually.
6. Preserve Canadian sovereignty over resources, permitting, public procurement, and strategic assets.
7. Keep humanitarian, emergency-response, public-health, and treaty-mandated cooperation outside punitive retaliation.
8. Use diversification as a parallel strategy so Canadian leverage grows as dependence on any single market falls.
9. Require transparent valuation so Canada receives comparable economic value for major strategic concessions.
10. Build provincial, state, Indigenous, municipal, labour, and private-sector participation into project design where relevant.
